such that for any trivial for every trivial dual Banach
G
-module
W
and (inhomogenous)
2
-cochain
\alpha\in L^{\infty}_{w*}(G^{2},W)
, there exists an (inhomogenous)
2
-cocycle
\alpha^{\prime}\in L^{\infty}_{w*}(G^{2},W)
such that
\|\alpha-\alpha^{\prime}\|\leq C_{2}\|\delta^{2}\alpha\|
.

Proof.

Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that there exists a sequence
\{W_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
of dual Banach spaces (all with trivial actions of
G
), and
2
-cocyles
\{\alpha_{n}\in L^{\infty}_{w*}(G^{2},W_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
with
\|\alpha_{n}\|=1
for every
n\geq 1
, such that for every sequence
\{\beta_{n}\in L^{\infty}_{w*}(G,W_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
with
\delta^{1}\beta_{n}=\alpha_{n}
for every
n\geq 1
, we have
\|\beta_{n}\|\geq n
. Consider the
\ell^{\infty}
direct sum
W\coloneq\oplus_{n}W_{n}
(which is a dual Banach space) and the
2
-cocycle
\alpha\coloneq\oplus_{n}\alpha_{n}
with
\|\alpha\|=1
. Then since
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{2}(G,W)=0
, that there exists
\beta\in L^{\infty}_{w*}(G,W)
with
\delta^{1}\beta=\alpha
, and let
\|\beta\|=C_{1}
. Note that the projections of
\beta
on
W_{n}
for
n>C_{1}
would give us
\beta_{n}\in L^{\infty}_{w*}(G,W_{n})
with
\delta^{1}\beta_{n}=\alpha_{n}
and
\|\beta_{n}\|\leq C_{1}
, contradicting our assumption. The proof of the second item is similar.
∎

We now use the universality of the constants
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
in
Lemma

4.2.10
to show the vanishing of
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{2}(G,\mathcal{V})
for a trivial Banach
{}^{*}G
-module, just like in
Proposition

4.2.9
.

Proposition 4.2.11
.

Suppose
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{2}(G,V)=0
and
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{3}(G,V)
is Hausdorff for every trivial dual Banach
G
-module
V
. Then
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{2}(G,\mathcal{V})=0
for every trivial asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}G
-module
\mathcal{V}
.

While the assumption that
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{2}(G,V)=0
and
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{3}(G,V)
is Hausdorff for
every
trivial dual Banach
G
-module
V
, a priori, seems stronger than the assumption that
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{2}(G,\mathbf{R})=0
and
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{3}(G,\mathbf{R})
is Hausdorff, we now show that the former is actually implied by the latter. Recall that a Banach space
V
is said to be
injective
if for any Banach space embedding
V\subset W
,
V
has a complement in
W
.

Proposition 4.2.12
.

Suppose
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{2}(G,\mathbf{R})=0
and
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{3}(G,\mathbf{R})
is Hausdorff. Then the image
\delta^{2}L^{\infty}(G^{2})
in
L^{\infty}(G^{3})
is injective.

Proof.

Note that the image
\delta^{1}L^{\infty}(G)
in
L^{\infty}(G^{2})
is closed, since
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{2}(G,\mathbf{R})=0
. In particular, this image is isomorphic to
L^{\infty}(G)/\mathbf{R}
, which is injective since
L^{\infty}(G)
and
\mathbf{R}
are injective. Next, since
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{3}(G,\mathbf{R})
is Hausdorff, the image
\delta^{2}L^{\infty}(G^{2})
is closed, and isomorphic to
L^{2}(G)/\delta^{1}L^{\infty}(G)
, which is injective since
L^{\infty}(G^{2})
and
\delta^{1}L^{\infty}(G)
are injective.
∎

Lemma 4.2.13
.

Suppose
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{2}(G,\mathbf{R})=0
and
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{3}(G,\mathbf{R})
is Hausdorff. Then for any trivial dual Banach
G
-module
V
,
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{2}(G,V)=0
and
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{3}(G,V)
is Hausdorff.

Proof.

Note (
Eq.

4.3
and
Eq.

4.1
) that
L^{\infty}_{w*}(G^{j},V)\cong L\left(V^{\flat},L^{\infty}(G^{j})\right)
. In this identification, the differential is just given by applying the scalar differential

\delta
to the image
L^{\infty}(G^{j})
. It follows that the complementing map given by
Proposition

4.2.12
provides a complementing map for the image
\delta^{2}L^{\infty}_{w*}(G^{2},V)
of
L^{\infty}_{w*}(G^{2},V)
in
L^{\infty}_{w*}(G^{3},V)
. In particular this image is closed and hence

\operatorname{H}_{b}^{3}(G,V)
is Hausdorff. Similarly, we can show that
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{2}(G,V)=0
as well.
∎

Combining
Lemma

4.2.13
and
Proposition

4.2.11
, we conclude that

Corollary 4.2.14
.

Suppose
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{2}(G,\mathbf{R})=0
and
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{3}(G,\mathbf{R})
is Hausdorff. Then
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{2}(G,\mathcal{V})=0
for any trivial dual Banach
{}^{*}G
-module
\mathcal{V}
.

This property of vanishing
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{2}(G,\mathbf{R})
and Hausdorffness of
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{3}(G,\mathbf{R})
will be very useful to us in
Section

6.3
, and in
Section

6.3
we shall study this property (called the “2½-property”) in more detail.

4.3
Amenable Actions and Cohomology of Subgroups

Recall that we had presented the asymptotic cohomology of
G
with coefficients in the dual asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}G
-module
\mathcal{V}
as asymptotic cohomology of the complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{2},\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{3},\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{4},\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{4}}

In this subsection, we shall relate the functorial descriptions in §
4.1
with
Definition

4.2.4
to obtain other complexes that can also be used to compute the same cohomology.

The first step is to interpret
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{\bullet}(G,\mathcal{V})
in a way that fits with
Proposition

4.1.13
. For this, we recall the following classical fact that follows from the Dunford-Pettis theorem: for a (standard) dual Banach space
E
,

B(L^{1}(G^{m})\times E)\cong L\left(L^{1}(G^{m}),E^{\sharp}\right)\cong L^{\infty}_{w*}(G^{m},E^{\sharp})

(4.3)

In fact, this goes through even when we consider the analogous asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}G
-modules. Denoting the ultrapower
{}^{*}L^{1}(G^{m})
by
\mathcal{L}^{1}(G^{m})
, we note that
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{m})
is a dual asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-module with predual
\mathcal{L}^{1}(({}^{*}G)^{m})
. In fact, it is more than just an asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-representation:
{}^{*}G
actually has a (true) internal action on
\mathcal{L}^{1}(({}^{*}G)^{m})
and its dual
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{m})
.

This allows us to construct
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{\bullet}(G,\mathcal{V})
starting from the
G
-cochain complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\infty}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\infty}(G^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\infty}(G^{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\infty}(G^{4})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{4}}

extending it internally to the asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-cochain complex (which, in this case, turns out to be a (true) internal cochain complex)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{4})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{4}}

and finally using the covariant functor
d\mapsto d_{*}
for the coefficient module
\mathcal{V}^{\flat}
(the predual of
\mathcal{V}
) to get

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}^{1}({}^{*}G)\times\mathcal{V}^{\flat})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}^{1}(({}^{*}G)^{2})\times\mathcal{V}^{\flat})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{L}^{1}(({}^{*}G)^{3})\times\mathcal{V}^{\flat})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{3}}

which, in turn, is seen to be the same as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{2},\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{3},\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{4},\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{4}}

The advantage of this reformulation of
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{\bullet}(G,\mathcal{V})
is that we can use
Lemma

4.1.14
to construct other asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-cochain complexes that compute the same cohomology
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{\bullet}(G,\mathcal{V})
. In view of this approach, we review some definitions and facts from
[
37
]
:

Definition 4.3.1
.

Let
S
be a regular
G
-space. A
conditional expectation

\mathfrak{m}:L^{\infty}(G\times S)\to L^{\infty}(S)
is a measurable norm one linear map such that

•

\mathfrak{m}(1_{G\times S})=1_{S}

•

For every
f\in L^{\infty}(G\times S)
and every measurable set
A\subset S
,
\mathfrak{m}(f\cdot 1_{G\times A})=\mathfrak{m}(f)\cdot 1_{A}
.

The
G
-action on
S
is said to be
Zimmer amenable
if there exists a
G
-equivariant conditional expectation
\mathfrak{m}:L^{\infty}(G\times S)\to L^{\infty}(S)
.

What we shall use is the following consequence of Zimmer amenability:

Proposition 4.3.2
.

Let
S
be a regular
G
-space with a Zimmer-amenable action of
G
. Then there exists a
G
-homotopy equivalence between the
G
-cochain complexes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\infty}(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\infty}(G^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\infty}(G^{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\infty}(G^{4})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{4}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\infty}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\infty}(S^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\infty}(S^{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{\infty}(S^{4})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{4}}

Extending this internally, if
S
a regular
G
-space with a Zimmer-amenable action of
G
, then this gives us an asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-homotopy equivalences between the asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-cochain complexes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{4})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{4}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}S)^{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}S)^{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}S)^{4})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{4}}

Now, since
(L^{1}(S^{m}))^{\sharp}=L^{\infty}(S^{m})
and
B(L^{1}(S^{m})\times E)\cong L\left(L^{1}(S^{m}),E^{\sharp}\right)\cong L^{\infty}_{w*}(S^{m},E^{\sharp})
, applying
Lemma

4.1.14
with
\mathcal{X}^{\bullet}=\mathcal{L}^{1}(({}^{*}G)^{\bullet})
,
\mathcal{Y}^{\bullet}=\mathcal{L}^{1}(({}^{*}S)^{\bullet})
and
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{V}^{\flat}
, we get

Theorem 4.3.3
.

Let
S
be a regular
G
-space with a Zimmer-amenable action of
G
. Then
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{\bullet}(G,\mathcal{V})
can be computed as the asymptotic cohomology of the asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-cochain complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}S,\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}S)^{2},\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}S)^{3},\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}S)^{4},\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{4}}

We now state some observations that follow immediately from
Theorem

4.3.3
, which we shall use later in
Proposition

6.1.12
in §
6
. Let
S
and
T
be two Zimmer-amenable regular
G
-spaces, so that by
Theorem

4.3.3
, we have an asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-homotopy equivalence between the asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-cochain complexes
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}S)^{\bullet},\mathcal{V})
and
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}T)^{\bullet},\mathcal{V})
given by
k^{\bullet}:\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}S)^{\bullet},\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}T)^{\bullet},\mathcal{V})
and
j^{\bullet}:\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}T)^{\bullet},\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}S)^{\bullet},\mathcal{V})
.

•

Let
\omega\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}T)^{m+1},\mathcal{V})^{\sim{}^{*}G}
be an asymptotic
m
-cocycle such that
Im(\omega)\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}G}
. Then
Im(j^{m}\omega)\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}G}
. This follows from the construction of
j^{m}
and
Proposition

4.1.2
.

•

Let
\omega\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}S)^{m+1},\mathcal{V})^{\sim{}^{*}G}
be an asymptotic
m
-cocycle such that
Im(k^{m}\omega)\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}G}
. Then, setting
\omega_{1}=j^{m}k^{m}\omega\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}S)^{m+1},\mathcal{V})^{\sim{}^{*}G}
, note that
Im(\omega_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}G}
. Furthermore, since
k^{\bullet}
and
j^{\bullet}
are asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-homotopy equivalences,
\omega
and
\omega_{1}
are asymptotically cohomologous, that is, there exists
\alpha\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{V})^{\sim{}^{*}G}
such that

\tilde{\omega}-\tilde{\omega}_{1}=\tilde{d}^{m}\tilde{\alpha}

(4.4)

Theorem

4.3.3
has the following two immediate corollaries which we shall use in §
5
and §
6
. The first of these is an asymptotic analogue of the classical result that amenable groups have vanishing bounded cohomology, and follows from the fact that if
G
is amenable, then the trivial space is Zimmer-amenable for
G
.

Corollary 4.3.4
.

Let
G
be an amenable group and
\mathcal{V}
be a dual asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}G
-module. Then for every
n\geq 1
,
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{n}(G,\mathcal{V})=0
.

The second corollary uses the fact that for a lattice
\Gamma
in a locally compact group
G
,
G
is Zimmer-amenable as a regular
\Gamma
-space. This serves as the starting point for an induction procedure to go from
\Gamma
to
G
which we describe in §
5
.

Corollary 4.3.5
.

Let
\Gamma\leq G
be a lattice in a locally compact group
G
, and let
\mathcal{W}
be a dual asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}\Gamma
-module. Then
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{\bullet}(\Gamma,\mathcal{W})
can be computed as the asymptotic cohomology of the asymptotic
{}^{*}\Gamma
-cochain complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{2},\mathcal{W})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{3},\mathcal{W})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{4},\mathcal{W})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{4}}

The next corollary uses the fact that for a closed subgroup
Q\leq G
(and in particular, when
Q=G
), and a closed amenable subgroup
P\leq G
, the space
G/P
is a regular
Q
-space that is Zimmer-amenable for the
Q
-action.

Corollary 4.3.6
.

Let
P\leq G
be a closed amenable subgroup of
G
,
Q
be a closed subgroup of
G
, and
\mathcal{V}
be a dual asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}G
-module. Then
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{\bullet}(Q,\mathcal{V})
can be computed as the asymptotic cohomology of the asymptotic
{}^{*}Q
-cochain complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}(G/P)),\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}(G/P))^{2},\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}(G/P))^{3},\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{3}}

The second corollary uses the fact that for a closed subgroup
Q\leq G
, the space
G/P
is a regular
Q
-space that is Zimmer-amenable for the
Q
-action.

5
The Induction Module

In the previous section, we noted (in
Corollary

4.3.5
) that for a lattice
\Gamma
in a Lie group
G
, the cohomology
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{\bullet}(\Gamma,\mathcal{W})
could be computed as the asymptotic cohomology of the asymptotic
{}^{*}\Gamma
-cochain complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{2},\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{3},\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{4},\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{4}}

We shall now apply an induction procedure to go from
{}^{*}\Gamma
-equivariance to
{}^{*}G
-equivariance, and shall construct an asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}G
-module
\mathcal{V}
such that
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{\bullet}(\Gamma,\mathcal{W})\cong\operatorname{H}_{a}^{\bullet}(G,\mathcal{V})
.

In §
5.1
, we begin by studying useful properties of an intermediary structure
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
that shall arise in the induction procedure worked out in §
5.2
. This structure is, upto infinitesimals, equal to the induced module
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
that we shall use, but has the additional useful feature of being equipped with a (true) internal action of
{}^{*}G
. A
1
-cohomology argument is used to pass between asymptotically equivariant maps in
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
and (truly) equivariant maps in
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
, which is a result we shall often use, especially in §
6.1
. Finally, the induction procedure is described in §
5.2
.

5.1
The
{}^{*}G
-action on
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}

Recall that the internal Banach space
\mathcal{W}=\prod_{\mathcal{U}}\mathfrak{u}(k_{n})
came with an internal map
\pi_{\Gamma}:{}^{*}\Gamma\times\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{W}
such that this map induced an action of
{}^{*}\Gamma
on
\tilde{\mathcal{W}}=\mathcal{W}_{b}/\mathcal{W}_{inf}
. Let
S
be an Zimmer-amenable regular
G
-space. For
m\geq 0
, consider the internal Banach space

\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})

equipped with the following internal
{}^{*}G
-action: for
g\in{}^{*}G
,
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})
,

(g\cdot f)(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=f(g^{-1}x_{1},\dots,g^{-1}x_{m})

for
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in{}^{*}S
. Clearly
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})
is invariant with respect to this
{}^{*}G
-action, and induces a
{}^{*}G
-action on
\tilde{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})
.

Consider the subsets
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})^{{}^{*}G}
of bounded
{}^{*}G
-fixed points of this action, and the subset
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}G}
of bounded asymptotically
{}^{*}G
-fixed points. Clearly,

\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})^{{}^{*}G}+\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{inf}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}G}

We shall now show that the containment goes through in the other direction too. That is,

Lemma 5.1.1
.

For every
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}G}
, there exists
\beta\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{inf}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})
such that
f-\beta\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})^{{}^{*}G}
. Moreover, the map
f\mapsto\beta
is induced from an internal map from
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})
to itself.

Proof.

Consider the internal map
\alpha:{}^{*}G\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})
defined as
\alpha(g)\coloneq g\cdot f-f
. Note that since
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}G}
,
Im(\alpha)\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{inf}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})
(hence
\|\alpha\|\in{}^{*}\mathbf{R}_{inf}
). Let
\alpha=\{\alpha_{n}\}_{\mathcal{U}}
where for every
n\in\mathbf{N}
,
\alpha_{n}:G\to L^{\infty}_{w*}(S^{m},\mathfrak{u}(k_{n}))
. Observe that each such
\alpha_{n}
is a bounded function that satisfies the (inhomogenous)
1
-cocycle condition. That is,
\alpha_{n}\in\operatorname{H}_{b}^{1}\left(G,L^{\infty}_{w*}(S^{m},\mathfrak{u}(k_{n}))\right)
. From
[
37
]
, we know that
L^{\infty}_{w*}(S^{m},\mathfrak{u}(k_{n}))
is a relatively injective Banach
G
-module, and hence
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{1}\left(G,L^{\infty}_{w*}(S^{m},\mathfrak{u}(k_{n}))\right)=0
. In fact, there exists a constant
C
(independent of
n
) such that for every
n\in\mathbf{N}
, there exists
\beta_{n}\in L^{\infty}_{w*}(S^{m},\mathfrak{u}(k_{n}))
with
\alpha_{n}(g)=g\cdot\beta_{n}-\beta_{n}
, with
\|\beta_{n}\|\leq C\|\alpha_{n}\|
. Set
\beta=\{\beta_{n}\}_{\mathcal{U}}
so that for
g\in{}^{*}G
,
\alpha(g)=g\cdot\beta-\beta
implying that
g\cdot(f-\beta)=f-\beta
. Since
\|\beta\|\leq C\|\alpha\|
,
\beta\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{inf}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})
. Note that the correspondence
f\mapsto\beta
is internal by construction.
∎

A special case of particular interest to us is the internal Banach space
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
. This space comes equipped with an internal
{}^{*}G
-action and an asymptotic
{}^{*}\Gamma
-action:

•

For
g\in{}^{*}G
and
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
, define
(g\cdot f)(x)=f(xg)
for
x\in{}^{*}G
. This makes
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
an internal
{}^{*}G
-representation.

•

Consider the internal map
\pi^{\prime}_{\Gamma}:{}^{*}\Gamma\times\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
defined as follows: for
\gamma\in{}^{*}\Gamma
and
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
, define

(\pi^{\prime}_{\Gamma}(\gamma)f)(x)=\pi_{\Gamma}(\gamma)f(\gamma^{-1}x)

where
x\in{}^{*}G
. This makes
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
into an asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}\Gamma
-module.

Remark 5.1.2
.

Note that
{}^{*}G
acts internally on
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
, while
{}^{*}\Gamma
does not act, through
\pi^{\prime}_{\Gamma}
, on
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
, but only induces an action on the quotient
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
.

Let us restrict to the subspace
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
comprising functions that are asymptotically
{}^{*}\Gamma
-equivariant. Note that
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
is
not
an internal space.

Lemma 5.1.3
.

The subset
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
is invariant under the internal action of
{}^{*}G
.

Consider the subspaces
\left(\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{{}^{*}G}
and
\left(\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{\sim{}^{*}G}
. Observe that the proof of
Lemma

5.1.1
goes through when restricted to asymptotically
{}^{*}\Gamma
-equivariant elements, giving us:

Corollary 5.1.4
.

For
v\in\left(\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{\sim{}^{*}G}
, there exists
w\in\left(\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{{}^{*}G}
such that
v-w\in\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
. Moreover, the map
v\mapsto w
is induced from an internal map from
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
to itself.

An element
f\in\left(\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{{}^{*}G}
satisfies the following two conditions:

•

For every
g\in{}^{*}G
and almost every
x\in{}^{*}G
,
f(xg)=f(x)
. In other words,
f
is an essentially constant function, where the constant value is an element of
\mathcal{W}_{b}
.

•

For
\gamma\in{}^{*}\Gamma
and almost every
x\in{}^{*}G
,
f(\gamma x)-\gamma f(x)\in\mathcal{W}_{inf}
(that is, the constant value of
f
is an element of
\mathcal{W}_{b})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
).

Thus,
f\in\left(\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{{}^{*}G}
can be represented as an element of
(\mathcal{W}_{b})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
.

Lemma 5.1.5
.

The internal map
e:\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(G,\mathcal{W})
defined as
e(w)(g)\coloneq w
for
w\in\mathcal{W}_{b}
and
g\in{}^{*}G
restricts to a bijection between
\mathcal{W}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
and
\left(\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{{}^{*}G}
.

Corollary

5.1.4
and
Lemma

5.1.5
together imply that, upto infinitesimals,
\left(\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{\sim{}^{*}G}
can be identified with
\mathcal{W}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
, and this bijection is (trivially)
{}^{*}G
-equivariant and induced from an internal map between
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
and
\mathcal{W}
.

We combine the results above to obtain a corollary that will be useful later in §
6
.

Proposition 5.1.6
.

Let
Q
be a closed subgroup of
G
. Let
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}\left(({}^{*}Q)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)^{\sim{}^{*}Q}
be such that
Im(f)\subseteq(\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma})^{\sim{}^{*}G}
. Then there exists
\beta\in\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}Q)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)
such that
f-\beta
is
{}^{*}Q
-fixed, and
Im(f-\beta)\subseteq\left(\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{{}^{*}G}=\mathcal{W}_{b}^{{}^{*}\Gamma}
. The map
f\mapsto\beta
is induced from an internal map from
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}Q)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)
to itself.

Proof.

Since
Im(f)\subseteq(\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma})^{\sim{}^{*}G}
,
Corollary

5.1.4
gives us
f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}\left(({}^{*}Q)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)^{\sim{}^{*}Q}
with
f-f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{inf}\left(({}^{*}Q)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)
and
Im(f^{\prime})\subseteq(\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma})^{{}^{*}G}=\mathcal{W}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
. The conclusion then follows from applying
Lemma

5.1.1
to
f^{\prime}
.
∎

Recall
Lemma

5.1.1
where an element in
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}G}
was shown to be corrected to get a truly
{}^{*}G
-fixed element in
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}S)^{m},\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}G}
. We shall now see a similar result with coefficients being
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
instead, which we shall use in §
5.2
. For
m\geq 0
, consider the internal space

\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)

The internal action of
{}^{*}G
on
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
can be extended to the following internal action of
{}^{*}G
on this space in the natural way: for
g,h\in{}^{*}G
,
F\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)
,

(g\cdot F)(g_{1},g_{2},\dots,g_{m})(x)=F(g^{-1}g_{1},\dots,g^{-1}g_{m})(xg)

For convenience, let us denote by

\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)

the space of internal maps in
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}_{in}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)
whose image is contained in
\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
. From
Lemma

5.1.3
, this space is invariant under the internal action of
{}^{*}G
, so denote by

\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{{}^{*}G}

the subspace of
{}^{*}G
-equivariant maps, and by

\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{\sim{}^{*}G}

the subspace of asymptotically
{}^{*}G
-equivariant maps. That is,
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{\sim{}^{*}G}
if for every
g\in{}^{*}G
,
g\cdot f-f\in\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)
.

Note that

\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{{}^{*}G}+\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{\sim{}^{*}G}

In other words, a perturbation of a
{}^{*}G
-equivariant function by an infinitesimal function is clearly an asymptotically
{}^{*}G
-equivariant function. We now show that the converse is true. That is, any asymptotically
{}^{*}G
-equivariant map is infinitesimally close to a
{}^{*}G
-equivariant map.

Proposition 5.1.7
.

For
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{\sim{}^{*}G}
, there exists
\beta\in\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)
such that
f-\beta\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{{}^{*}G}
(that is,
f-\beta
is (truly)
{}^{*}G
-equivariant). The map
f\mapsto\beta
is induced from an internal map from
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)
to itself.

Proof.

The argument is exactly as in the proof of
Lemma

5.1.1
, except that now we use the fact that
L^{\infty}_{w*}(G^{m},L^{\infty}_{w*}(G,\mathfrak{u}(k_{n})))
is relatively injective as a
G
-module.
∎

In conclusion,

\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{{}^{*}G}+\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)=\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{\sim{}^{*}G}

5.2
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
and the Eckmann-Shapiro Induction

Consider the space
\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
. We shall now show that, upto infinitesimals, this space can be identified with the internal Banach space
\mathcal{V}\coloneq\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
where
D
is a Borelian left fundamental domain of
\Gamma
in
G
. This will then be used to serve as the coefficients to define the asymptotic cohomology
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{\bullet}(G,\mathcal{V})
of
G
.

Consider the internal map

\theta:\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})

given by restriction of a function to
{}^{*}D
. That is, for
f=\{f_{n}\}_{\mathcal{U}}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
,

\theta f=\{f_{n}|_{D}\}_{\mathcal{U}}

(5.1)

In the other direction, consider the internal map

\zeta:\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})

\zeta f(g)\coloneqq\pi_{\Gamma}(\gamma)f(z)

(5.2)

where
g=\gamma z
for
\gamma\in{}^{*}\Gamma
and
z\in D
. Observe that
\theta\cdot\zeta
is the identity on
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
. As for
\zeta\cdot\theta
,

Lemma 5.2.1
.

For any
f\in\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
,
(\zeta\cdot\theta)(f)-f\in\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
.

Proof.

Since
f\in\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
, we note that for
\gamma\in{}^{*}\Gamma
and
z\in{}^{*}D
,
f(\gamma z)-\pi_{\Gamma}(\gamma)f(z)\in\mathcal{W}_{inf}
.
∎

Furthermore, since
\theta
maps
\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
to
\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
, and
\zeta
maps
\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
to
\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
,

Lemma 5.2.2
.

The internal maps
\theta
and
\zeta
induce bijections

\tilde{\theta}:\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}/\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\to\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})

\tilde{\zeta}:\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})\to\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}/\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})

with
\tilde{\zeta}=\tilde{\theta}^{-1}
.

Henceforth we shall restrict
\theta
to
\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
. We shall now define an internal map

\pi_{G}:{}^{*}G\times\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})

\pi_{G}(g)(f)(z)\coloneqq\pi_{\Gamma}(\gamma)f(x)

(5.3)

where
zg=\gamma x
for
\gamma\in{}^{*}\Gamma
and
x\in{}^{*}D
. Note that
{}^{*}G\times{}^{*}D\to{}^{*}D
given by
(g,z)\mapsto x
, where
zg=\gamma x
, defines an internal right action of
{}^{*}G
on
{}^{*}D
. So the above map
\pi_{G}
in
Eq.

5.3
can be denoted as

\pi_{G}(g)(f)(z)\coloneqq\pi_{\Gamma}(\gamma)f(zg)

This map
\pi_{G}
induces an action of
{}^{*}G
on
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
, which we shall denote
\tilde{\pi_{G}}
. In particular, note that this gives the internal Banach space
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
the structure of an asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}G
-module, with the asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-representation being
\pi_{G}
as defined above.

Lemma 5.2.3
.

The maps
\tilde{\theta}
and
\tilde{\zeta}
are
{}^{*}G
-equivariant.

Proof.

Note that while we have an internal action of
{}^{*}G
on
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
that is invariant on
\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
, the internal map
\pi_{G}
is not an action of
{}^{*}G
on
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
. Nevertheless, let
f\in\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
and
g\in{}^{*}G
. Then
\pi_{G}(g)(\theta f)(z)=\pi_{\Gamma}(\gamma)(\theta f)(x)=\pi_{\Gamma}(\gamma)f(x)
where
zg=\gamma x
. Also,
(\theta gf)(z)=f(zg)=f(\gamma x)
. Since
f\in\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
,
f(\gamma x)-\pi_{\Gamma}(\gamma)f(x)\in\mathcal{W}_{inf}
. Thus,
\theta gf-\pi_{G}(g)\theta f\in\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
. A similar argument hold for
\zeta
as well.
∎

Remark 5.2.4
.

Consider the space
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}G}
of asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-fixed elements. The restrictions of the maps
\zeta
and
\theta
to
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}G}
and
\left(\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{\sim{}^{*}G}
allows us to identify these two spaces upto infinitesimals. Furthermore, from
Corollary

5.1.4
and
Lemma

5.1.5
, we see that, upto infinitesimals,
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}G}
can be identified with
\mathcal{W}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
.

One of the advantages of defining and working with
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
(instead of
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
) is that, not only is it an asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}G
-module, but is also easily seen to be dual, which we explicitly describe below. Consider the internal Banach space
\mathcal{L}^{1}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W}^{\flat})
constructed as

\mathcal{L}^{1}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W}^{\flat})\coloneqq\prod_{\mathcal{U}}L^{1}\left(D,(\mathfrak{u}(k_{n}))^{\flat}\right)

where
L^{1}\left(D,(\mathfrak{u}(k_{n}))^{\flat}\right)
is the Bochner-Lebesgue space of Bochner-integrable functions from
D
to
(\mathfrak{u}(k_{n}))^{\flat}
. Note that
L^{\infty}(D,\mathfrak{u}(k_{n}))
is the dual space of
L^{1}(D,(\mathfrak{u}(k_{n}))^{\flat})
, so in particular, an element of
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
is an internal linear map from
\mathcal{L}^{1}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W}^{\flat})
to
{}^{*}\mathbf{R}
. We now have an explicit dual pairing can be used to construct the predual asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-action given
\pi_{G}
. For
f=\{f_{n}\}_{\mathcal{U}}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
and
\eta=\{\eta_{n}\}_{\mathcal{U}}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W}^{\flat})
, define
\langle f,\eta\rangle
as

\langle f,\eta\rangle=\left\{\int_{D}\langle f_{n}(x),\eta_{n}(x)\rangle dx\right\}_{\mathcal{U}}

This defines an internal pairing

\langle,\rangle_{\mathcal{U}}:\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})\times\mathcal{L}^{1}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W}^{\flat})\to{}^{*}\mathbf{R}

that induces a pairing between the
\mathbf{R}
-spaces
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
and
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{1}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W}^{\flat})
. The space
\mathcal{L}^{1}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W}^{\flat})
comes with an internal map

\pi_{G}^{\flat}:{}^{*}G\times\mathcal{L}^{1}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W}^{\flat})\to\mathcal{L}^{1}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W}^{\flat})

such that:

•

The map
\tilde{\pi}_{G}
is contragredient to
\tilde{\pi}_{G}^{\flat}
, that is, for
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
,
\eta\in\mathcal{L}^{1}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W}^{\flat})
and
g\in{}^{*}G
,

\langle\pi_{G}(g)f,\eta\rangle-\langle f,\pi_{G}^{\flat}(g)\eta\rangle\in{}^{*}\mathbf{R}_{inf}

•

The internal map
\pi_{G}^{\flat}
induces an action of
{}^{*}G
, denoted
\tilde{\pi}_{G}^{\flat}
, on the quotient
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{1}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W}^{\flat})
.

Thus,

Proposition 5.2.5
.

The internal Banach space
(\pi_{G},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W}))
is a dual asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}G
-module with predual
\mathcal{L}^{1}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W}^{\flat})
.

We now have the dual asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}G
-module
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
and an internal map

\pi_{G}:{}^{*}G\times\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{V}

that induces an action of
{}^{*}G
on
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}
. This allows us to define the asymptotic cohomology of
G
with coefficients in
\mathcal{V}
as in §
4.1
, as the cohomology of the complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{V}}^{{}^{*}G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{V})^{{}^{*}G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{2},\mathcal{V})^{{}^{*}G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{d}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{d}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{d}^{1}}

Theorem 5.2.6
.

For every
m\geq 0
,
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{m}(G,\mathcal{V})\cong\operatorname{H}_{a}^{m}(\Gamma,\mathcal{W})
.

The first step towards proving
Theorem

5.2.6
involves a bijection between
{}^{*}\Gamma
-invariants in
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{W})
, and
{}^{*}G
-invariants in
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{V}\right)
. After that, we shall use the internal maps
\theta:\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
and
\zeta:\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})
(defined in (
Eq.

5.1
) and (
Eq.

5.2
)) to pass between
\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
and
\mathcal{V}
.

Let
\tilde{\alpha}\in\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{W})^{{}^{*}\Gamma}
, and let
\alpha\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{W})
be an internal map that induces
\tilde{\alpha}
(note that
\alpha\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
). Define the internal map

A_{\alpha}:({}^{*}G)^{m}\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})

A_{\alpha}(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})(x)\coloneq\alpha(xg_{1},\dots,xg_{m})

Firstly it is clear that
A_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)
. Furthermore,

Proposition 5.2.7
.

For every
\tilde{\alpha}\in\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{W})^{{}^{*}\Gamma}
, the map
A_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)
takes values in
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
and is
{}^{*}G
-equivariant.

Proof.

For
g,x\in{}^{*}G
,

A_{\alpha}(gg_{1},\dots,gg_{m})(x)=\alpha(xgg_{1},\dots,xgg_{m})=A_{\alpha}(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})(xg)

This proves that
A_{\alpha}
is
{}^{*}G
-equivariant. Next, for
\gamma\in{}^{*}\Gamma
,

A_{\alpha}(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})(\gamma x)=\alpha(\gamma xg_{1},\dots,\gamma xg_{m})

Since
\tilde{\alpha}\in\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{W})^{{}^{*}\Gamma}
, this means that

\alpha(\gamma xg_{1},\dots,\gamma xg_{m})-\pi_{\Gamma}(\gamma)\alpha(xg_{1},\dots,xg_{m})\in\mathcal{W}_{inf}

Hence
A_{\alpha}(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})(\gamma x)-\pi_{\Gamma}(\gamma)\left(A_{\alpha}(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})(\gamma x)\right)\in\mathcal{W}_{inf}
. This shows that
A_{\alpha}(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
.
∎

Thus, given a
{}^{*}\Gamma
-equivariant map
\tilde{\alpha}\in\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{W})
, we obtain an internal
{}^{*}G
-equivariant map
A_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)
that takes values in
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
.

Conversely, suppose we have an internal
{}^{*}G
-equivariant map
A\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)
that takes values in
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
. Define the internal map

\alpha_{A}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{W})

\alpha_{A}(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})\coloneqq A(x^{-1}g_{1},\dots,x^{-1}g_{m})(x)

for
x\in{}^{*}G
. Note that since
A
is
{}^{*}G
-equivariant, the map
x\mapsto A(x^{-1}g_{1},\dots,x^{-1}g_{m})(x)
is essentially constant in
\mathcal{W}
, making the above well-defined.

Lemma 5.2.8
.

Given an internal
{}^{*}G
-equivariant map
A\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)
that takes values in
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
, the internal map
\alpha_{A}
as defined above induces the map
\tilde{\alpha}_{A}
that is
{}^{*}\Gamma
-equivariant. That is,
\tilde{\alpha}_{A}\in\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{W})^{{}^{*}\Gamma}
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma\in{}^{*}\Gamma
, then since
A
is
{}^{*}G
-equivariant,

\alpha_{A}(\gamma g_{1},\dots,\gamma g_{m})=A(x^{-1}\gamma g_{1},\dots,x^{-1}\gamma g_{m})(x)=A(x^{-1}g_{1},\dots,x^{-1}g_{m})(\gamma x)

Since
A
takes values in
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
, we know that

A(x^{-1}g_{1},\dots,x^{-1}g_{m})(\gamma x)-\pi_{\Gamma}(\gamma)\left(A(x^{-1}g_{1},\dots,x^{-1}g_{m})(x)\right)\in\mathcal{W}_{inf}

Thus,
\alpha_{A}(\gamma g_{1},\dots,\gamma g_{m})-\pi_{\Gamma}(\gamma)\alpha_{A}(g_{1},\dots,g_{m})\in\mathcal{W}_{inf}
, proving that
\tilde{\alpha}_{A}\in\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{W})^{{}^{*}\Gamma}
.
∎

Furthermore, the correspondences

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\mapsto A_{\alpha}

(5.4)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapsto\alpha_{A}

(5.5)

are clearly inverses of each other, thus giving a bijection between internal maps
\alpha\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{W})
that are asymptotically
{}^{*}\Gamma
-equivariant, and internal
{}^{*}G
-equivariant maps
A\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)
that take values in
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.2.6
.

For
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\small{\geq 0}}
, it is sufficient to show a bijection between the quotients
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{m+1},\mathcal{W})^{{}^{*}\Gamma}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{m+1},\mathcal{V})^{{}^{*}G}
that commutes with the differentials
\tilde{d}
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}G)^{m+1},\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
and let

A_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m+1},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\right)

be its lift (as in (
Eq.

5.4
)) that is asymptotically
{}^{*}G
-equivariant and takes values in
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
. Composing
A_{\alpha}
with
\theta
, we get

\theta\cdot A_{\alpha}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m+1},\mathcal{V}\right)^{\sim{}^{*}G}

In the other direction, for an internal map
A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m+1},\mathcal{V}\right)^{\sim{}^{*}G}
, we first compose
\zeta
(defined in (
Eq.

5.2
)) with
A^{\prime}
to get
\zeta\cdot A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m+1},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)
, where
\zeta\cdot A^{\prime}
is asymptotically
{}^{*}G
-equivariant. Let
A\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left(({}^{*}G)^{m+1},\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{{}^{*}G}
be the
{}^{*}G
-equivariant map infinitesimally close to
\zeta\cdot A^{\prime}
(as guaranteed by
Proposition

5.1.7
).

Now we can descend to
\Gamma
by defining the internal map

\alpha_{A}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{m+1},\mathcal{W})

\alpha_{A}(g_{0},\dots,g_{m})\coloneqq A(x^{-1}g_{0},\dots,x^{-1}g_{m})(x)

Since
A
is
{}^{*}G
-equivariant, from
Lemma

5.2.8
we conclude that
\alpha_{A}
is asymptotically
{}^{*}\Gamma
-equivariant. Thus, we have a bijection between the quotients
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{m+1},\mathcal{W})^{{}^{*}\Gamma}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}(({}^{*}G)^{m+1},\mathcal{V})^{{}^{*}G}
that commutes with the differentials
\tilde{d}.

∎

5.3
Internal Contraction and Fixed Points

Recall that we have the dual asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}G
-module
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
with the asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-action of
{}^{*}G
given by
\pi_{G}
as in (
Eq.

5.3
). The map
\pi_{G}
can be studied in terms of two internal maps: an internal right
{}^{*}G
-action
{}^{*}G\times{}^{*}D\to{}^{*}D
of
{}^{*}G
on
{}^{*}D
given by
(g,z)\mapsto zg
, and another internal twisting map
{}^{*}G\times{}^{*}D\to{}^{*}\Gamma
given by
(g,z)\mapsto\gamma
(here
\gamma\in{}^{*}\Gamma
and
x\in{}^{*}D
are such that
zg=\gamma x
). The latter map is then composed with
\pi_{\Gamma}
to give
\pi_{G}(g)(f)(z)\coloneqq\pi_{\Gamma}(\gamma)f(x)
as in (
Eq.

5.3
).

We now see a continuity property of the asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-action
\pi_{G}
on
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
. Observe that the internal map
(g,z)\mapsto\pi_{\Gamma}(\gamma)
is internally measurable (and internally locally constant), and as for the internal (true) action of
{}^{*}G
on
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
given by
(g\cdot f)(z)\coloneq f(x)
, this is internally continuous, but with respect to the internal
L^{2}
-norm defined on
f=\{f_{n}\}_{\mathcal{U}}
as follows:

\|f\|_{2}^{2}\coloneq\left\{\int_{D}\|f_{n}(x)\|^{2}dx\right\}_{\mathcal{U}}

Lemma 5.3.1
.

The dual asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}G
-module
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
is internally continuous with respect to the internal
L^{2}
-norm on
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
.

We shall refer to internal continuity with respect to the
L^{2}
-norm on
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
as
internal
L^{2}
-continuity
. The main consequence of this property that we shall use is the following: let
g^{(1)},g^{(2)},\dots
be a sequence of elements of
{}^{*}G
(with
g^{(m)}=\{g_{n}{(m)}\}_{\mathcal{U}}
) such that the internal limit
\lim\limits_{m\to\infty}g^{(m)}=\{\lim\limits_{m\to\infty}g_{n}^{(m)}\}_{\mathcal{U}}=g\in{}^{*}G
. Then for
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
,
\lim\limits_{m\to\infty}f(g^{(m)})=f(g)
(where the limit is now with respect to the internal
L^{2}
-norm). A particular instance of an internal limit we shall use is given by
contraction
of elements.

Definition 5.3.2
.

Let
g,h\in G
. We say that
h
is contracted by
g
(or
g
contracts
h
) if
\lim\limits_{m\to\infty}g^{-m}hg^{m}=1
. Let
g=\{g_{n}\}_{\mathcal{U}}
and
h=\{h_{n}\}_{\mathcal{U}}
be elements of
{}^{*}G
. We say
h

is internally contracted by
g
(or
g
internally contracts
h
) if for every
n\in\mathcal{U}
,

\lim\limits_{m\to\infty}g_{n}^{-m}h_{n}g_{n}^{m}=1

In other words,
g
internally contracts
h
if the sequence
g^{-m}hg^{m}
has internal limit being the identity in
{}^{*}G
.

Definition 5.3.3
.

An internal subgroup of the ultrapower
{}^{*}G
is called an
internally amenable subgroup
if it is of the form
\prod_{\mathcal{U}}H_{n}
where
H_{n}\leq G
is a closed amenable subgroup for every
n\in\mathbf{N}
.

Remark 5.3.4
.

Suppose
g,h\in G
such that
g
contracts
h
. Then the closed subgroup
<g,h>
generated by
g
and
h
is an amenable subgroup of
G
(refer Propositions
6.5
and
6.17
in
[
16
]
). In particular, this implies that if
g,h\in{}^{*}G
is such that
g
internally contracts
h
, then the internal subgroup
<g,h>
generated by
g
and
h
in
{}^{*}G
is an internally amenable subgroup of
{}^{*}G
.

To use the full power of internal contraction and the internal
L^{2}
-continuity of the asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-action
\pi_{G}
, our first step is to “correct” the asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-action
\pi_{G}
to get a true action when restricted to an internally amenable subgroup. Note that the imperfection is in the map
\pi_{G}^{\prime}
, so consider the map
\alpha:{}^{*}G\times{}^{*}D\to\prod_{\mathcal{U}}U(k_{n})
with
\alpha(g,z)\coloneq\pi_{\Gamma}(\gamma)
. Note that
\alpha
is a measurable map, and for every
g_{1},g_{2}\in{}^{*}\Gamma
and
z\in{}^{*}D
,

\|\alpha(g_{1},z)\alpha(g_{2},zg_{1})-\alpha(g_{1}g_{2},z)\|\in{}^{*}\mathbf{R}_{inf}

(5.6)

Observe that this looks similar to the very classical question of Ulam stability, but with a twist provided by the action of
G
on
D
. We consider the following definitions which are analogues of uniform stability in the context of such twists:

Definition 5.3.5
.

Let
G
be a locally compact, second countable group, and
X
be a non-singular
G
-space. A measurable map
\psi:G\times X\to U(n)
is called a
twisted
homomorphism of
G
(with respect to
D
) if for every
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
and almost every
z\in X
,

\psi(g_{1}g_{2},z)=\psi(g_{1},z)\psi(g_{2},zg_{1})

For
\epsilon>0
(the defect), a measurable map
\phi:{}G\times X\to U(n)
is called a twisted
\epsilon
-homomorphism of
G
(with respect to
D
) if for almost every
g_{1},g_{2}\in G
and almost every
z\in X

\|\phi(g_{1},z)\phi(g_{2},zg_{1})-\phi(g_{1}g_{2},z)\|\leq\epsilon

The following claim essentially retraces the arguments used in §
3
(where we use the logarithm map to obtain an asymptotic cocycle, and use the vanishing of cohomology to diminish defect).

Claim 5.3.6
.

There exists
\epsilon>0
small enough, and a constant
C
such that for an amenable subgroup
H
of
G
and any twisted
\epsilon
-homomorphism
\alpha:G\times D\to U(n)
, there exists a measurable map
\alpha_{H}:H\times D\to U(n)
of
H
such that for every
h\in H
and almost every
z\in D
,
\|\alpha(h,z)-\alpha_{H}(h,z)\|\leq C\epsilon
, and for every
h_{1},h_{2}\in H
and
z\in D
,

\alpha_{H}(h_{1},z)\alpha_{H}(h_{2},zh_{1})=\alpha_{H}(h_{1}h_{2},z)

Proof.

As in §
3
, consider a sequence
\{\alpha_{n}:H\times D\to U(k_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbf{N}}
and its ultraproduct
\alpha
, which is an asymptotic twisted homomorphism with defect
\epsilon\in{}^{*}\mathbf{R}_{inf}
. Define the internal map

\omega:{}^{*}H\times{}^{*}H\times{}^{*}D\to\prod_{\mathcal{U}}\mathfrak{u}(k_{n})

\omega(h_{1},h_{2},z)={}_{\epsilon}\log\left(\alpha(h_{1},z)\alpha(h_{2},zh_{1})\alpha(h_{1}h_{2},z)^{-1}\right)

As
\mathfrak{u}(k_{n})=\mathcal{W}
, note that we can regard
\omega
as an element of
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}H\times{}^{*}H\times{}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
, or equivalently,
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}H)^{2},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W}))
. Equipping
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
with an asymptotic action
\rho_{\alpha}:{}^{*}H\times\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
of
{}^{*}H
given by
\rho_{\alpha}(h)(f)(z)\coloneq\alpha(h,z)f(zh)\alpha(h,z)^{-1}
, making it a dual asymptotic
{}^{*}H
-module. One can check that the map
\omega
satisfies the condition to be an (inhomogenous) asymptotic
2
-cocycle in
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{2}(H,\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W}))
. Now since
H
is amenable and
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
is a dual asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}H
-module, we know (from
Corollary

4.3.4
) that
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{2}(H,\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W}))=0
, implying that there exists
\beta\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}H,\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W}))
such that
\alpha\cdot_{\epsilon}\exp{\beta}
is an
o_{\mathcal{U}}(\epsilon)
-twisted homomorphism (note that
\alpha\cdot_{\epsilon}\exp{\beta}
is measurable). Repeating this process (as in defect diminishing), we obtain a measurable map
\alpha_{H}^{\prime}:H\times D\to U(n)
such that for almost every
h_{1},h_{2}\in H
and almost every
z\in D
, we have
\alpha_{H}^{\prime}(h_{1},z)\alpha_{H}^{\prime}(h_{2},zh_{1})=\alpha_{H}^{\prime}(h_{1}h_{2},z)
. From Theorem B
9
(p.200) in
[
51
]
, we conclude that there exists
\alpha_{H}
as desired.
∎

Remark 5.3.7
.

The same argument also goes through for internally amenable subgroups
\mathcal{H}
by applying
5.3.6
internally.

Lemma 5.3.8
.

Let
\mathcal{H}\leq{}^{*}G
be an internally amenable subgroup of
{}^{*}G
. Then there exists an internal map
\pi_{\mathcal{H}}:\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
such that

•

For every
h_{1},h_{2}\in H
,
\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(h_{1}h_{2})=\pi_{H}(h_{1})\pi_{H}(h_{2})
. In other words,
\pi_{\mathcal{H}}
is a (true) internal action of
\mathcal{H}
on
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
.

•

For every
f\in\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
and
h\in\mathcal{H}
,
\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(h)f-\pi_{G}(h)f\in\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
.

•

The internal action
\pi_{\mathcal{H}}
of
\mathcal{H}
is internally
2
-continuous.

Proof.

Consider the internal map
\alpha:{}^{*}G\times{}^{*}D\to\prod_{\mathcal{U}}U(k_{n})

\alpha(g,z)\coloneq\pi_{\Gamma}(\gamma)

where
\gamma x=zg
for
\gamma\in{}^{*}\Gamma
and
x\in{}^{*}D
. Since
\alpha
is an asymptotic twisted homomorphism of
G
, from
Remark

5.3.7
, there exists an internally measurable map
\alpha_{\mathcal{H}}:\mathcal{H}\times{}^{*}D\to\prod_{\mathcal{U}}U(k_{n})
as in
5.3.6
.

Define
\pi_{\mathcal{H}}:\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
by

\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(g)(f)(z)\coloneq\alpha_{\mathcal{H}}(g,z)f(x)

whereas always,
x\in{}^{*}D
such that
zg=\gamma x
for
\gamma\in{}^{*}\Gamma
. Then
\pi_{\mathcal{H}}
is a (true) internal action of
\mathcal{H}
on
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
, and for every
f\in\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
and
h\in\mathcal{H}
,
\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(h)f-\pi_{G}(h)f\in\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
. Since
\pi_{\mathcal{H}}
is an internal action of
\mathcal{H}
that is internally measurable, it is also internally
2
-continuous.
∎

Lemma 5.3.9
.

Let
x,y\in{}^{*}G
such that
x
internally contracts
y
. Suppose
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
is such that
\pi_{G}(x)f-f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{inf}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
(in other words,
x
fixes
\tilde{f}
). Then
\pi_{G}(y)f-f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{inf}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
, that is,
y
too fixes
\tilde{f}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be the closure of the subgroup generated by
x
and
y
. By
Remark

5.3.4
,
\mathcal{H}
is an internally amenable subgroup of
{}^{*}G
. By
Lemma

5.3.8
, consider the correction
\pi_{\mathcal{H}}
whose restriction to
\mathcal{H}
is a (true) internally
2
-continuous action of
\mathcal{H}
on
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
. Now
\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(x)f-f\in\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
, and let
f_{\mathcal{H}}\in\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
such that
f-f_{\mathcal{H}}\in\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
and
\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(x)f_{\mathcal{H}}=f_{\mathcal{H}}
.

Now we have

\|\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(y)f_{H}-f_{H}\|_{2}=\|\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(y)\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(x^{-m})f_{\mathcal{H}}-\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(x^{-m})f_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{2}

Since
\pi_{\mathcal{H}}
too acts unitarily,

\|\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(y)\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(x^{-m})f_{\mathcal{H}}-\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(x^{-m})f_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{2}=\|\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(x^{m})\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(y)\pi_{H}(x^{-m})f_{\mathcal{H}}-f_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{2}

Since
\pi_{\mathcal{H}}
is a (true) internal action of
{\mathcal{H}}
,
\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(x^{m})\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(y)\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(x^{-m})=\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(x^{m}yx^{-m})
, and so

\|\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(x^{m})\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(y)\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(x^{-m})f_{\mathcal{H}}-f_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{2}=\|\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(x^{m}yx^{-m})f_{\mathcal{H}}-f_{\mathcal{H}}\|_{2}

Since
\pi_{\mathcal{H}}
is internally
2
-continuous and
y
is internally contracted by
x
, we conclude that
\pi_{\mathcal{H}}(y)f_{\mathcal{H}}=f_{\mathcal{H}}
, implying that
\pi_{G}(y)f-f\in\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
.
∎

We can apply the above results in a slightly more general setting, which we shall develop into an internal
Mautner’s Lemma
in Subection §
6.1
.

Definition 5.3.10
.

Let
T\leq G
be a closed subgroup and
M>0
. The group
G
is said to be
M
-boundedly generated by
T
-contracted elements
if any element
g\in G
, there exist
s_{1},\dots,s_{m}\in G
with
m\leq M
, such that
g=s_{1}s_{2}\dots s_{m}
, and each
s_{i}\in G
is contracted by some element of
T
. More generally, for a family
\mathfrak{T}
of closed subgroups of
G
, the group
G
is said to be
boundedly generated by
\mathfrak{T}
-contracted elements
if there exists
M>0
such that
G
is
M
-boundedly generated by
T
-contracted elements for every
T\in\mathfrak{T}
.

Remark 5.3.11
.

Suppose
G
is boundedly generated by
\mathfrak{T}
-contracted elements for a family
\mathfrak{T}
of subgroups. Let
\mathcal{T}=\prod_{\mathcal{U}}T_{n}
be an internal subgroup of
{}^{*}G
, with
T_{n}\in\mathfrak{T}
for every
n\in\mathbf{N}
. Observe that there exists
M>0
such that any
g\in{}^{*}G
can be expressed as
g=s_{1}s_{2}\dots s_{m}
such that each
s_{i}\in{}^{*}G
is internally contracted by some element of
\mathcal{T}
.

With this definition,
Lemma

5.3.9
implies the following corollary:

Corollary 5.3.12
.

Suppose
G
is boundedly generated by
\mathfrak{T}
-contracted elements, and let
\mathcal{T}=\prod_{\mathcal{U}}T_{n}
be an internal subgroup of
{}^{*}G
, with
T_{n}\in\mathfrak{T}
for every
n\in\mathbf{N}
. Then if
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})^{\sim\mathcal{T}}
, then
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}G}
(that is, if
f
is asymptotically fixed by all elements of
\mathcal{T}
, then it is asymptotically
{}^{*}G
-fixed).

Proof.

Let
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})^{\sim\mathcal{T}}
and
g\in{}^{*}G
. Let
g=s_{1}s_{2}\dots s_{m}
as in
Remark

5.3.11
. Since
f
is asymptotically fixed by every element of
\mathcal{T}
,
Lemma

5.3.9
implies that it is asymptotically fixed by each
s_{i}
. In particular,
f
is asymptotically fixed by
g
. Thus,
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}G}
.
∎

6
Vanishing of
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{2}(\Gamma,\mathcal{W})

In this section, we combine the results of the previous sections to prove our main result about uniform stability of lattices in semisimple groups. In §
6.1
, we build further tools specialized to
G
being semisimple groups, which in turn shall be used in §
6.2
. We begin with some structure properties of semisimple groups with regard to the notions of bounded generation by contracting elements, as discussed in §
5.3
, and use it prove an asymptotic version of the Mautner property and an asymptotic double ergodicity theorem with coefficients. In §
6.2
, we study the Property-
G(\mathcal{Q}_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{2})
, which is an assumption on
G
that would allow us to build on the results of §
6.1
to prove our main theorem, namely that
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{2}(G,\mathcal{V})=0
. We then conclude the section by studying Property-
G(\mathcal{Q}_{1},\mathcal{Q}_{2})
in more detail in §
6.3
, and list out a large class of semisimple groups that satisfy it, thus making our main result applicable to them.

6.1
Asymptotic Mautner Property and Ergodicity

In this subsection and henceforth, we shall work with
G
being a semisimple group of the form
G=\prod^{k}_{i=1}{\mathbf{G}_{i}}(K_{i})
where for
1\leq i\leq k
,
K_{i}
is a local field, and
\mathbf{G}_{i}
is a connected, simply connected, almost
K_{i}
-simple group. Our goal is to use the results developed in the previous sections to prove that
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{2}(\Gamma,\mathcal{W})=\operatorname{H}_{a}^{2}(G,\mathcal{V})=0
(recall that
\mathcal{W}
is the ultraproduct
\prod_{\mathcal{U}}\mathfrak{u}(k_{n})
which is an asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}\Gamma
-module obtained from the asymptotic homomorphism we start with, while
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
is the asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}G
-module obtained by the induction procedure in
Section

5.2
).

Recall
Definition

5.3.10
of bounded generation by contracted elements. We shall now see that
G
is boundedly generated by
\mathfrak{T}
-contracted elements, for the subgroup family
\mathfrak{T}
being the maximal tori of the radicals of proper parabolic subgroups of
G
.

Proposition 6.1.1
.

Let
G=\mathbf{G}(K)
be a connected, simply connected, almost
K
-simple
K
-isotropic group over local field
K
, and let
\mathfrak{T}
be the family of maximal tori of the solvable radicals of proper parabolic subgroups of
G
. Then
G
is boundedly generated by
\mathfrak{T}
-contracted elements.

Proof.

Let
T
be a maximal torus contained in the solvable radical of a proper parabolic subgroup
Q
of
G
. It is sufficient to show that for some
M>0
,
G
is
M
-boundedly generated by
T
-contracted elements. This is because the same bound
M
works for conjugates of
T
, and upto conjugation, the group
G
has only finitely many parabolic subgroups.

Let
Q=L\cdot R_{u}(Q)
be the Levi decomposition of
Q
, where
L
is the Levi subgroup and
R_{u}(Q)
is the unipotent radical of
Q
. Then every element of
R_{u}(Q)
is contracted by some element of
T
. In fact, the same holds in the case of the opposite parabolic
Q^{-}
, that is, every element of
R_{u}(Q^{-})
too is contracted by some element of
T
. Hence it is sufficient to show that
G
is boundedly generated by
\Delta\coloneqq R_{u}(Q)(K)\cup R_{u}(Q^{-})(K)
, that is, to show that there exists
\ell>0
such that
G=\Delta^{\ell}
.

Firstly, note that
G
is indeed generated by
\Delta
(
[
34
, 1.5.4]
), and so, there exists
k>0
so that the product map
{\Delta}^{k}\to G
is a dominant
K
-morphism. From
[
45
, Proposition 3.3]
, we conclude that
\Omega\coloneqq\Delta^{\ell}
contains an open neighborhood of the identity. Let
T
be a maximal
K
-split torus of
G
contained in
Q\cap Q^{-}
. For any
K
-defined unipotent subgroup
U\subset G
which is normalized by
T
, we have

U(K)=\bigcup_{t\in T(K)}t(U(K)\cap\Omega)t^{-1}

Since
T(K)
normalizes
\Delta
(and hence also
\Omega
), we see that
U(K)\subseteq\Omega
. In particular, for a
K
-defined minimal parabolic subgroup
P\subset Q
containing
T
, we conclude that
R_{u}(P)(K)\subset\Omega
.

Let
D=T\cdot R_{u}(P)
. Then there exists a compact subset
E\subset G
so that

G=E\cdot D(K)=E\cdot T(K)\cdot R_{u}(P)(K)

Since
\Omega
is open and generates
G
, there exists
s>0
such that
E\subset\Omega^{s}
. Hence it is sufficient to show that there exists
t>0
such that
T(K)\subset\Omega^{t}
.

There exists
N>0
such that for any root
\alpha
of
T
with coroot
\alpha^{\vee}
(recall that for a root
\alpha:T(K)\to K
, its dual
\alpha^{\vee}:K\to T(K)
is such that
\alpha^{\vee}(\alpha)=2
),
\alpha^{\vee}(t)
for
t\in K^{\times}
can be written as a product of
N
elements of
\Delta
(for instance,
N=6
when
G=SL_{n}
). In particular, this means that every element of
T(K)
can be written as a product of
Nn
elements of
\Delta
(where
n
is the rank of
G
). This concludes the proof.
∎

In fact,
Proposition

6.1.1
can be immediately extended to semisimple groups as well, as long as we ensure that the projection of the tori to each factor is non-trivial.

Corollary 6.1.2
.

Let
G
be a semisimple group of the form
G=\prod^{k}_{i=1}{\mathbf{G}_{i}}(K_{i})
where for
1\leq i\leq k
,
K_{i}
is a local field, and
\mathbf{G}_{i}
is a connected, simply connected, almost
K_{i}
-simple
K_{i}
-isotropic group, and let
\mathfrak{T}
be the family of maximal tori of the radicals of parabolic subgroups of
G
of the form
Q=\prod_{i=1}^{k}Q_{i}
, where each
Q_{i}
is a proper parabolic subgroup of
{\mathbf{G}_{i}}(K_{i})
. Then
G
is boundedly generated by
\mathfrak{T}
-contracted elements.

Combining
Corollary

5.3.12
with
Corollary

6.1.2
, we immediately get the following:

Corollary 6.1.3

(Asymptotic Mautner Property)
.

Let
G
and a parabolic subgroup
Q\leq G
be as in
Corollary

6.1.2
, and let
N\leq G
be the radical of
Q
. Then
\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}N}=\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}G}
.

Corollary

5.3.12
and
Corollary

6.1.2
can also be used to get an internal analogue of ergodicity (and double ergodicity) with coefficients as in
[
37
]
. We recall the following classical definitions for comparison:

Definition 6.1.4
.

For a locally compact second countable group
G
and a regular
G
-space
S
, the
G
-action on
S
is said to be
ergodic
if any measurable
G
-invariant function
f:S\to\mathbf{R}
is essentially constant. The
G
-action on
S
is said to be
doubly ergodic
if the diagonal
G
-action on
S\times S
is ergodic.

There is no difference if
\mathbf{R}
above is replaced by any dual separable Banach space: the
G
-action on
S
is ergodic (resp., double ergodic) iff for any dual separable Banach space
E
, any weak-* measurable
G
-
invariant
map
f:S\to E
(resp,
f:S\times S\to E
) is essentially constant. Indeed the underlying Borel structures are isomorphic. Even if
E
is not separable, it suffices that it admits a weak-* measurable (for instance dual linear) injection into a separable dual, since we can then apply ergodicity in the latter space. Here is an illustration of this phenomenon:

Claim 6.1.5
.

Let
f\colon G\to L^{\infty}_{w*}(D,\mathfrak{u}(k_{n}))
be a
G
-invariant weak-* measurable map. Then
f
is essentially constant.

Proof.

The
G
-action on itself is ergodic, so that this follows from the previous discussion after noticing that
L^{\infty}_{w*}(D,\mathfrak{u}(k_{n}))
admits a dual linear injection into
L^{2}(D,\mathfrak{u}(k_{n}))
, which is dual and separable. This injection is the dual of the natural map from
L^{2}(D,\mathfrak{u}(k_{n}))
to
L^{1}(D,\mathfrak{u}(k_{n}))
, which is defined because
D
has finite measure.
∎

Note that
6.1.5
immediately implies the following (wherein no
G
-structure is considered on
\mathcal{V}
):

Claim 6.1.6
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}\left({}^{*}G,\mathcal{V}\right)
be
{}^{*}G
-invariant (that is, for every
x\in{}^{*}G
and almost every
g\in{}^{*}G
,
F(xg)=F(g)
). Then
F
is essentially constant. ∎

A classical example of a doubly ergodic action for a semisimple group
G
(as in
Corollary

6.1.2
) is its action on
G/P
for
P
being a minimal parabolic subgroup of
G
(which implies that the action of
G
on
G/Q
is doubly ergodic for any parabolic subgroup
Q
of
G
).

We shall now use
Corollary

5.3.12
with
Corollary

6.1.2
to get a much stronger double ergodicity result for asymptotically
{}^{*}G
-
equivariant
maps. Classically, this is referred to as double ergodicity
with coefficients
(
[
37
, Chapter 4.11]
):

A
G
-action on a regular
G
-space
S
is said to be
ergodic with coefficients in a Banach
G
-module
E
if any weak-
*
measurable
G
-equivariant map
f:S\to E
is essentially constant. Note that this is a much stronger condition than ergodicity; it does not even hold for the transitive action of
G
on itself. The action is called
doubly ergodic with coefficients
if the corresponding condition holds for the diagonal
G
-action on
S\times S
.

In our setting, the goal is to show that for
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}\left({}^{*}(G/P)^{2},\mathcal{V}\right)^{\sim{}^{*}G}
, there exists
F\in\mathcal{V}_{b}
so that
f-F\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{inf}\left({}^{*}(G/P)^{2},\mathcal{V}\right)
. In other words, we would like to prove an asymptotic version of double ergodicity of the action of
G
on
G/P
with coefficients being the asymptotic Banach
{}^{*}G
-module
\mathcal{V}
. The crucial difference with
6.1.5
is that we involve the
G
-structure on
\mathcal{V}
this time.

Remark 6.1.7
.

We caution the reader that no (non-trivial) action whatsoever can be ergodic with
arbitrary
coefficients. Therefore, the nature of the asymptotic
G
-module
\mathcal{V}
intervenes in the result. Specifically, the fact that it involves a finite
G
-invariant measure on
D
is used and it enters the proof through our appeal to the asymptotic Mautner’s lemma (
Corollary

6.1.3
), which ultimately relies on the
L^{2}
-topology used in
Lemma

5.3.9
. That
L^{2}
-continuity argument, introduced in §
5.3
, is not available in the absence of a finite invariant measure (and indeed the statements can fail in that absence).

We begin with a series of short claims that shall be used in the proof of
Theorem

6.1.11
. The first of these states that it is sufficient to work with
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}\left({}^{*}(G/A),\mathcal{V}\right)^{\sim{}^{*}G}
for
A=P\cap wPw^{-1}
for some
w\in G
.

Claim 6.1.8
.

There exists
w\in G
such that for
A=P\cap wPw^{-1}
, the map

G/A\to G/P\times G/P

gA\mapsto(gP,gwP)

is a measure space isomorphism respecting the action of
G
.

Proof.

The result is a consequence of
[
9
, Theorem 3.13 and Corollary 3.15]
.
∎

The next claim is a special case of the asymptotic Mautner’s lemma
Corollary

6.1.3
for
A
.

Claim 6.1.9
.

Let
A
be as in
6.1.8
. Then
\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}A}=\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}G}
.

Proof.

This is again a consequence of
Corollary

6.1.2
and
Corollary

5.3.12
, since
A
contains a maximal torus of
G
.
∎

Now we obtain a correction of
asymptotically

{}^{*}G
-invariant maps to
truly

{}^{*}G
-invariant maps:

Claim 6.1.10
.

Suppose
f\in\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}\left({}^{*}G,\mathcal{V}\right)
is such that for every
x\in{}^{*}G
and almost every
g\in{}^{*}G
,
f(xg)-f(g)\in\mathcal{V}_{inf}
. Then there exists
F\in\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}\left({}^{*}G,\mathcal{V}\right)
such that
F-f\in\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}\left({}^{*}G,\mathcal{V}\right)
and for every
x\in{}^{*}G
and almost every
g\in{}^{*}G
,
F(xg)=F(g)
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as in
Lemma

5.1.1
, but now using the fact that
\mathcal{L}^{\infty}\left({}^{*}G,\mathcal{V}\right)
is the ultraproduct of relatively injective Banach
G
-modules
L^{\infty}_{w*}(G,L^{\infty}_{w*}(D,\mathfrak{u}(k_{n})))
.
∎

With the above statement at hand, we now prove that:

Theorem 6.1.11

(Double Ergodicity with coefficients)
.

Let
G
be as in
Corollary

6.1.2
. For
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}\left({}^{*}(G/P)^{2},\mathcal{V}\right)^{\sim{}^{*}G}
, there exists
v\in\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}G}
so that
f-v\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{inf}\left({}^{*}(G/P)^{2},\mathcal{V}\right)
.

Proof.

From
6.1.8
, we know that this is equivalent to proving that for
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}\left({}^{*}(G/A),\mathcal{V}\right)^{\sim{}^{*}G}
, there exists
v\in\mathcal{V}_{b}
so that
f-v\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{inf}\left({}^{*}(G/A),\mathcal{V}\right)
. Define
f^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}\left({}^{*}G,\mathcal{V}\right)
as

f^{\prime}(g)\coloneqq\pi_{G}(g)^{-1}f(g\,{}^{*}A)
. Since
\pi_{G}
is an asymptotic
{}^{*}G
-action and
f
is asymptotically
{}^{*}G
-equivariant, for every
x\in{}^{*}G
and almost every
g\in{}^{*}G
,

f^{\prime}(xg)-f^{\prime}(g)\in\mathcal{V}_{inf}

In other words,
f^{\prime}
, by construction, is asymptotically
{}^{*}G
-invariant. From
6.1.10
, there exists
F\in\mathcal{L}_{b}^{\infty}\left({}^{*}G,\mathcal{V}\right)
such that
F-f\in\mathcal{L}_{inf}^{\infty}\left({}^{*}G,\mathcal{V}\right)
and for every
x\in{}^{*}G
and almost every
g\in{}^{*}G
,
F(xg)=F(g)
. Now from
6.1.6
,
F
is essentially constant, and hence there exists
v\in\mathcal{V}_{b}
such that for almost every
g\in{}^{*}G
,
f^{\prime}(g)-v\in\mathcal{V}_{inf}
. Thus, for almost every
g\in{}^{*}G
,

f(g{}^{*}A)-\pi_{G}(g)v\in\mathcal{V}_{inf}

We now claim that
v\in\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}A}
. Then, by
6.1.9
, we have
v\in\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}G}
, allowing us to conclude that for almost every
g\in{}^{*}G
,
f(g{}^{*}A)-v\in\mathcal{V}_{inf}
. Hence it is left to prove that
v\in\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}A}
.

Define the pullback
\hat{f}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}\left({}^{*}G,\mathcal{V}\right)
by
\hat{f}(g)\coloneqq f(g{}^{*}A)
, and fix a measurable section
s:G/A\to G
to define a measure isomorphism

G/A\times A\to G

(gA,a)\mapsto s(gA)a

The above isomorphism can be defined internally to get an internal measure isomorphism
{}^{*}(G/A)\times{}^{*}A\to{}^{*}G
(with the internal section map which we continue denoting
s
for simplicity), and we have that for almost every
g{}^{*}A\in{}^{*}(G/A)
and almost every
a\in{}^{*}A
,

\hat{f}(s(g{}^{*}A)a)-\pi_{G}(s(g{}^{*}A)a)v\in\mathcal{V}_{inf}

Since
\hat{f}(s(g{}^{*}A)a)=\hat{f}(s(g{}^{*}A))
, the above simplifies to the following: for almost every
g{}^{*}A\in{}^{*}(G/A)
and almost every
a\in{}^{*}A

\pi(s(g{}^{*}A))v-\pi_{G}(s(g{}^{*}A)a)v\in\mathcal{V}_{inf}

Now we fix some
g{}^{*}A\in{}^{*}(G/A)
so that for this
g{}^{*}A
, we have that for almost every
a\in{}^{*}A
,

\pi_{G}(s(g{}^{*}A))v-\pi_{G}(s(g{}^{*}A)a)v\in\mathcal{V}_{inf}

Since
\pi_{G}
is an asymptotic action of
{}^{*}G
,
\pi_{G}(s(g{}^{*}A)a)v-\pi_{G}(s(g{}^{*}A))\pi_{G}(a)v\in\mathcal{V}_{inf}
. And so, we conclude that, for almost every
a\in{}^{*}A
,

v-\pi_{G}(a)v\in\mathcal{V}_{inf}

This means that there exists an internal subset
\mathcal{A}=\{A^{\prime}_{n}\}_{\mathcal{U}}
of
{}^{*}A
with
A^{\prime}_{n}
being a conull subset of
A
for
n\in\mathcal{U}
, such that
v\in\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim\mathcal{A}}
. But note that
\mathcal{A}\cdot\mathcal{A}={}^{*}A
(since if
A_{n}^{\prime}
is a co-null subset of the locally compact group
A
, then
A_{n}^{\prime}A_{n}^{\prime}=A
). Hence
v\in\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}A}
as claimed.
∎

Theorem

6.1.11
tells us that for any
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}\left({}^{*}(G/P)^{2},\mathcal{V}\right)^{\sim{}^{*}G}
the induced map
\tilde{f}\in\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\infty}\left({}^{*}(G/P)^{2},\mathcal{V}\right)^{{}^{*}G}
is essentially constant. This is the asymptotic analogue of the classical result that the
G
-action on
G/P
is doubly ergodic with coefficients in a suitable
G
-module.

We conclude this subsection by showing that
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{2}(Q,\mathcal{V})=0
for a proper parabolic subgroup
Q
of
G
as in
Corollary

6.1.2
such that
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{2}(Q,\mathbf{R})=0
and
\operatorname{H}_{b}^{3}(Q,\mathbf{R})
is Hausdorff. We begin with the following proposition that is an application of
Corollary

6.1.3
:

Proposition 6.1.12
.

Let
G
and a parabolic subgroup
Q\leq G
be as in
Corollary

6.1.2
. Let
\omega\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}Q)^{3},\mathcal{V})^{\sim{}^{*}Q}
be an asymptotic
2
-cocycle for
Q
with coefficients in
\mathcal{V}
. Then there exists an asymptotic
2
-cocycle
\omega^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}Q)^{3},\mathcal{V})^{\sim{}^{*}Q}
for
Q
that takes values in
\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}G}
such that

\tilde{\omega}-\tilde{\omega}^{\prime}=\tilde{d}^{1}\tilde{\alpha_{1}}

where
\alpha_{1}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}Q)^{2},\mathcal{V})^{\sim{}^{*}Q}
.

Proof.

Let
N
be the unipotent radical of
Q
, which is a normal amenable closed subgroup of
Q
. Since
N
is amenable,
Theorem

4.3.3
tells us that
\operatorname{H}_{a}^{\bullet}(Q,\mathcal{V})
can be computed as the asymptotic cohomology of the asymptotic
{}^{*}Q
-cochain complex

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}(Q/N),\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}(Q/N))^{2},\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}(Q/N))^{3},\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d^{3}}

Let
k^{\bullet}:\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}Q)^{\bullet},\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}(Q/N))^{\bullet},\mathcal{V})
and
j^{\bullet}:\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}(Q/N))^{\bullet},\mathcal{V})\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(({}^{*}Q)^{\bullet},\mathcal{V})
be the asymptotic
{}^{*}Q
-homotopy equivalences. Consider
k^{2}\omega\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}(Q/N))^{3},\mathcal{V})^{\sim{}^{*}Q}
. Since
N
is normal in
Q
,
Im(k^{2}\omega)\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}N}
. By
Corollary

6.1.3
, since
\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}N}=\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}G}
, this implies that
Im(k^{2}\omega)\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}G}
. The conclusion then follows from (
Eq.

4.4
).
∎

Thus, the obstacle to the asymptotic
2
-cocycle
\omega\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}Q)^{3},\mathcal{V})^{\sim{}^{*}Q}
being an asymptotic
2
-coboundary is the asymptotic
2
-cocycle
\omega^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}Q)^{3},\mathcal{V})^{\sim{}^{*}Q}
that takes values in
\mathcal{V}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}G}
. We shall now see how to handle this using assumptions on
Q
.

Consider the asymptotic
2
-cocycle
\omega^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}Q)^{3},\mathcal{V})^{\sim{}^{*}Q}
for
Q
that takes values in
\mathcal{V}^{\sim{}^{*}G}
. The first step is to correct
\omega^{\prime}
to an element
\omega^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}Q)^{3},\mathcal{W})^{{}^{*}Q}
with
Im(\omega^{\prime\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{W}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
. Recall the internal map
\theta:\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})\to\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}D,\mathcal{W})
as defined in
Eq.

5.1
.

Lemma 6.1.13
.

Given
\omega^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}Q)^{3},\mathcal{V})^{\sim{}^{*}Q}
with
Im(\omega^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{V}^{\sim{}^{*}G}
, there exists

\omega^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\infty}_{b}(({}^{*}Q)^{3},\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W}))^{{}^{*}Q}

with
Im(\omega^{\prime\prime})\subseteq\left(\mathcal{L}^{\infty}({}^{*}G,\mathcal{W})^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}\right)^{{}^{*}G}=\mathcal{W}_{b}^{\sim{}^{*}\Gamma}
such that
